### Grammar Notes
Two common ways to talk about the future are:
I will go to London tomorrow (future simple)
I am going to go to London tomorrow (“to be going to“)
These different forms communicate different ideas.
One common use of the future simple is to communicate that we have just
this moment decided to do something. For example, if I say “It’s getting
cold; I’ll close the window“, I am communicating that I have just this
moment decided to close the window.
We can also use the future simple to make a prediction. For example, if I
use “to be going to”, not the future simple. If you decide to watch a film
tonight, and then later you tell a friend what you have decided to do, you

**Q:** Give me an example, please. “To be going to“ can also be used to make a prediction, but a prediction using information we already have. For example, if I say “Oh, look at the sky; I think it’s going to rain“, I am predicting rain because I can see that the sky is dark and cloudy now. Another common use of “to be going to“ is to communicate a future intention; something that we have already decided to do. For example, if I say “I’m going to buy a new car“, I am communicating that I have already decided to buy a new car; it is my intention to buy one.?

## Lesson 80

### Vocabulary
- **Emphatic “do”**

- **just**

- **pleasure**

### Grammar Notes
I do speak English!                  I do like this book!
As you already know, we use the auxiliary verb “do“ for the present
simple, but only in questions and negative sentences; it is not used in the
positive. For example, we say “Do you speak English?“ and “I do not speak
English“, but we say “I speak English“, with no auxiliary “do“.
However, when we want to be emphatic about something, we can also
use “do“ in a positive sentence. For example, if someone says to you “You
don’t speak English“, you can reply “Yes I do speak English!“ In situations
like this, we put a heavy stress on the word “do“.
Reply to this sentence using the emphatic form in the past, please: He
didn’t cycle home yesterday.                                     Yes he did
Remember that we only use the emphatic “do“ with the present and past
simple. With other tenses, we just stress the first auxiliary verb, or the
negative word in the verb. For example, “Yes I can speak English well!“
Reply to these sentences using emphatic forms, please:
You can’t read quickly.                                   Yes I can read quickly!
The weather’s been good.                   No the weather has not been good!
She doesn’t love her family.                       Yes she does love her family!
He came to school early.                    No he didn’t come to school early!
We shouldn’t tell him the news.               Yes we should tell him the news!
The word “fair“ has five different meanings, which are as follows: ___________
(fair hair), ____________ (industrial and agricultural fairs, for example),
__________ (just), __________ (moderate), and __________ (fun fair).

### Grammar Notes
The difference between the words “what“ and “whatever“ is that we use
the word “what“ in a limited sense, whereas we use the word “whatever“
in a more unlimited sense. Other examples of this kind of thing are: “when
– whenever“; “where – wherever“; “who – whoever“.
We can form verbs from certain adjectives or nouns by adding the letters
“en“. For example, from the adjective “dark“ we can make the verb
“to darken“, which means “to make darker“ or “to get darker“. Other
examples are “short – shorten“, “wide – widen“ etc.

We form the perfect continuous tenses by using the verb “have“, the

action up to a particular point in the past. We are “measuring“ how long

means that I started cooking and then twenty minutes later she arrived.

### Grammar Notes
The structure “to have something done“ communicates that we do not do
the action ourselves, but that somebody else does it for us. For example,
“I do not cut my hair myself; I have my hair cut by the hairdresser“. Instead
of saying “John is going to repair my computer for me“, we can say “I am
going to have my computer repaired by John“.
The verb “save“ can mean “to keep for the future”, “to rescue”, “to
protect”, and “to spend less”.

tradesmen are bakers, butchers, builders etc.

a trip to the shops, a trip around the world etc.

### Grammar Notes
The most important difference between “tell“ and “say“ is that after “tell“
we indicate the person we are speaking to, but after “say“ we usually do
use “tell“ to report an order, not “say“. For example: He told me to go
downstairs.
He said me to go away
The verb “to earn“ generally means “to receive money for work done“,
whereas the verb “to gain“ generally means “to get more of something“.
For example, we can gain strength, time, friends etc., or we can gain
money through a business transaction.

**Q:** Do you usually write your plans in a diary?
**A:** Yes, I usually write my … ~ No, I don’t usually write my …

**Q:** Do you think it’s a good idea for teenagers to plan their futures whilst still?

**Q:** at school?
**A:** Yes, I think ... ~ No, I don’t think ...

**Q:** Why or why not?

**Q:** without the interference of their parents?
**A:** Yes, I think ... ~ No, I don’t think ...

**Q:** Why or why not?

### Dictation
> Some people/ have the manners of a pig./ Although he has lain in bed/ for several hours,/ he is still awake./ They did not notice/ the strange-looking house./ When people owe us a lot of money,/ we ought really to have a piece of paper/ with their signature on it/ and the amount they owe./ The servant took great pride/ in his work at the palace./ The lorry ran over some glass,/ and so we had to get out/ and change/ one of the wheels

### Grammar Notes
English has two basic types of auxiliary verb: primary auxiliaries and
modal auxiliaries (or “modals“).
We use the auxiliary “do“ for the present simple and past simple, for
example when we say “I don’t speak Spanish“ or “Did she eat the pasta?“
John is able to speak French.                             John can speak French
Perhaps he works in a bank.                   He may (or might) work in a bank
It is necessary for me to go to bed now.                   I must go to bed now
You are not allowed to smoke in this building.		          You can’t (or mustn’t)
After a modal, we put the infinitive without “to“. For example, we say “I
must go“, and not “I must to go“. The only exception to this rule is “ought“.
We say, for example, “I should call my mum soon“, but if we express the
same idea with “ought“, we say “I ought to call my mum soon“.

### Q&A Drill
**Q:** What are the two basic types of auxiliary verb in English?
**A:** The two basic types of auxiliary verb in English are primary auxiliaries and modal auxiliaries There are three primary auxiliaries: “be“, “have“ and “do“. We use them to make basic verb structures.

**Q:** What are the primary auxiliaries?
**A:** The primary auxiliaries are the verbs “be“, “have“ and “do“ We use the auxiliary “be“ to make the continuous tenses, for example

**Q:** when we say “I am speaking English now“. We also use it for the passive voice, for example when we say “This book was printed in England“.?

**Q:** When do we use the auxiliary “be“?
**A:** We use the auxiliary “be“ to make the continuous tenses and the passive voice

**Q:** Give me an example, please. We use the auxiliary “have“ for the perfect tenses, for example when we?
**A:** I am speaking English now. This book was printed in England.

**Q:** say “He has gone to Scotland“.?

**Q:** When do we use the auxiliary “have“?
**A:** We use the auxiliary “have“ for the perfect tenses

**Q:** When do we use the auxiliary “do“?
**A:** We use the auxiliary “do“ for the present simple and past simple

**Q:** Give me an example, please. There are ten common modals: “can“, “could“, “will“, “would“, “may“, “might“, “shall“, “should“, “must“ and “ought“. Modals normally express ideas about necessity or possibility.?
**A:** I don’t speak Spanish. Did she eat the pasta?

**Q:** What are the ten common modals?
**A:** The ten common modals are “can“, “could“, “will“, “would“, “may“, “might“, “shall“, “should“, “must“ and “ought“

**Q:** What ideas do modals normally express?
**A:** Modals normally express ideas about necessity or possibility Now, I will give you a sentence, and you say a sentence with the same smoke in this building

**Q:** What do we put after a modal?
**A:** We put the infinitive without “to“ after a modal

**Q:** Which modal is the only exception to this rule?
**A:** “Ought“ is the only exception to this rule

**Q:** Give me an example, please.?
**A:** I ought to see a doctor

**Q:** What does the word “set“ mean?
**A:** The word “set“ means “...“

**Q:** How long is the time set for a game of football?
**A:** The time set ... is 90 minutes; that is, 45 minutes each way

**Q:** What kind of life do you think is better: a life where you have a set time for doing everything, such as eating, sleeping etc., or a life where you do?

**Q:** these things whenever you want?
**A:** I think a life where you … is better than a life where you …

**Q:** What did I set down on the table when I came into the room at the?

**Q:** beginning of the lesson?
**A:** You set your book down on the table when you ...

**Q:** If your watch isn’t showing the right time, what do you have to do?
**A:** If my watch …, I have to set it right

**Q:** What time is sunrise at the moment?
**A:** Sunrise is at about … at the moment

**Q:** And what time is sunset?
**A:** Sunset is at about …

**Q:** What do you consider to be the duty a person owes to the society in?

**Q:** which they live?
**A:** I consider that the duty ... is to be honest, hard-working etc.

**Q:** What are the duties of a policeman?
**A:** The duties ... are to give people help and information, catch criminals etc.

**Q:** Do policemen wear their uniforms when they’re off duty?
**A:** No, policemen don’t wear ...; they only wear them when they’re on duty

**Q:** If you brought a foreign car into this country, would you have to pay duty?

**Q:** on it?
**A:** Yes, if I ..., I’d have to ... ~ No, if I ..., I wouldn’t have to ...

**Q:** And if you didn’t pay, what’d happen?
**A:** If I didn’t pay, they might make me pay a fine, and even take the car away from me

**Q:** What are the various ways of learning a language?
**A:** The various ways ... are at school with a teacher, going to the country where the language is spoken, practising with a friend, listening to the radio, watching TV etc.

**Q:** Is there much entertainment for teenagers in your home town?
**A:** Yes, there is a lot of … in my home town ~ No, there isn’t much … in my home town

**Q:** Is the weight of these two chairs about equal?
**A:** Yes, the weight of ...

**Q:** Would you find it interesting to operate a factory machine?
**A:** Yes, I’d find it ... ~ No, I wouldn’t find it ...

**Q:** Why or why not?

**Q:** Have you ever had an operation in hospital?
**A:** Yes, I’ve had … ~ No, I’ve never had …

### Grammar Notes
We generally use the words “elder“ and “eldest“ instead of “older“ and
“oldest“ when speaking about people in the same family. However, we
cannot say “elder than“. For example, we cannot say “My sister is elder
than me“; we must say “My sister is older than me“.
The difference between “to let“ and “to rent“ is that “to let“ means to
lend something in exchange for money, whereas “to rent“ means to
borrow something in exchange for money.

### Q&A Drill
**Q:** When we are speaking about time, the word “by“ can mean “at some point before“ or “not later than“. For example, “I will be home by midnight“ means that I will arrive home at some point before midnight, or possibly at midnight, but certainly not later than midnight.?

**Q:** Give me a sentence containing the words “by the time“.?
**A:** By the time she gets here, dinner will be ready

**Q:** What does “by the time“ mean in that sentence?
**A:** “By the time“ in that sentence means at some point between now and when she gets here By the time you are ninety years old, do you suppose your hair will be

**Q:** grey?
**A:** Yes, by the time I’m ninety years old, I suppose my …

**Q:** Will your English have improved by this time next month?
**A:** Yes, my English will …

**Q:** Can you speak English as well as you speak your own language?
**A:** No, I can’t speak English as well as I speak my own language; I speak it worse than my own language

**Q:** Do you take English lessons on Sunday as well as during the week?
**A:** Yes, I take ... ~ No, I don’t take...

**Q:** What does that last question mean in other words?
**A:** That last question means “You take English lessons during the week, but do you also take English lessons on Sunday?“

**Q:** What’s the difference between the words “older“ and “elder“?
**A:** The difference between ... is that we generally use the word “elder“ when speaking about people in the same family, and we cannot say “elder than”

**Q:** Have you got an elder brother?
**A:** Yes, I’ve got … ~ No, I haven’t got …

**Q:** Are you the eldest in your family?
**A:** Yes, I’m ... ~ No, I’m not ...

**Q:** Who is?

**Q:** What’s the difference between “to let“ and “to rent“?
**A:** The difference between “to let“ and “to rent“ …

**Q:** Supposing you had a house or a flat to let, how would you advertise it?
**A:** Supposing I had …, I’d advertise it in the newspaper or on the internet

**Q:** What’s the average monthly rent for a small flat in this town?
**A:** The average ... is … about ...

**Q:** Do you ever rent DVDs to watch at home?
**A:** Yes, I sometimes … ~ No, I never … We can sometimes say “to rent out“, instead of “to let“.

**Q:** Is it common in your country for people to rent out rooms in their house –?

**Q:** to students, for example?
**A:** Yes, it’s common in my country … ~ No, it isn’t common in my country …

**Q:** What are the three forms of “fall“?
**A:** The three forms of “fall“ are “fall, fell, fallen“

**Q:** Have you ever fallen out of bed in the middle of the night?
**A:** Yes, I’ve fallen … ~ No, I’ve never fallen …

**Q:** What’s the past of the verb “to choose“?
**A:** The past of the verb “to choose“ is “chose“

**Q:** Did you choose the clothes you’re wearing yourself?
**A:** Yes, I chose the clothes I’m wearing myself ~ No, I didn’t choose the clothes I’m wearing myself; someone else chose them for me

**Q:** Why do you suppose football is such a popular game?
**A:** I suppose football is such a popular game because … there is so little water

**Q:** Is a soldier’s uniform provided for him by the government?
**A:** Yes, a soldier’s uniform is …

**Q:** How do parents provide for their children?
**A:** Parents … by making sure they have all the things they need in life

**Q:** If you had just bought a house and then someone told you there was a ghost in it, what would you do, providing, of course, you believed in?

**Q:** ghosts?
**A:** If I had just bought a house and then someone told me there was a ghost in it, I’d …, providing I believed in ghosts In that last sentence, instead of using the word “providing“, we could

**Q:** have used “provided“. Both forms have the same meaning.?

**Q:** Are you going to go straight home after the lesson?
**A:** Yes, I’m going to go ... ~ No, I’m not going to go ...

**Q:** If you came to some crossroads while driving a car and, instead of?

**Q:** stopping, you kept straight on, what might happen?
**A:** If I came to ... I kept straight on, an accident might happen

**Q:** How many days does a fortnight consist of?
**A:** A fortnight consists of fourteen days

**Q:** Why do we have to place an egg carefully on the table?
**A:** We have to ... because it could easily break

**Q:** In which month does the academic year commence in your country?
**A:** The academic year … in my country

### Dictation
> The plural of mouse is mice./ He got a bad throat/ from speaking too much./ The colonies in America/ later developed/ into a large nation./ The TV was too loud,/ so we turned it down./ They just could not understand/ the reason for their tiredness./ Some people like living alone;/ others hate it./ In my opinion,/ the kitchen is too small./ There were several copies of the book/ in the library./ I cannot cycle any further;/ I am too tired.

### Q&A Drill
**Q:** What’s my hand doing?
**A:** Your hand is appearing and disappearing (from under the table)

**Q:** Does it appear to you that people in the world are becoming happier or?

**Q:** less happy?
**A:** It appears to me that ...

**Q:** Why?

**Q:** Do you judge people by appearances or do you wait until you get to?

**Q:** know them well?
**A:** I judge people by appearances ~ I don’t judge people by appearances; I wait until I get to know them well

**Q:** Which way of judging is fairer?
**A:** Judging people after getting to know them well is fairer

**Q:** If we double the number 25, what do we get?
**A:** If we …, we get 50

**Q:** How wide is a double bed?
**A:** A double bed is about double the width of a single bed Yes, I have … ~ No, I don’t have …

**Q:** When children get into trouble, do they sometimes tell lies in order to get?

**Q:** themselves out of trouble?
**A:** Yes, when children …

**Q:** When you are reading a book in your own language and you see a word you don’t know the meaning of, do you take the trouble to look it up in a?

**Q:** dictionary or do you just keep on reading?
**A:** When I’m reading ..., I take … ~ When I’m reading …, I don’t take ...; I just …

**Q:** Do dreams sometimes trouble you at night?
**A:** Yes, dreams sometimes trouble me at night ~ No, dreams never trouble me at night

**Q:** What do you think is the trouble with modern life?
**A:** I think the trouble …

**Q:** When an adjective ends in a single consonant preceded by a single vowel, the consonant is doubled when we form the comparative and superlative. For example, “big – bigger than – the biggest“.?

**Q:** When do we double the final consonant of an adjective in forming its?

**Q:** comparative and its superlative?
**A:** We double the final ... when it ends in a single consonant preceded by a single vowel

**Q:** Give me an example, please.?
**A:** thin – thinner than – the thinnest

**Q:** When an adjective ends in a consonant plus “y“, the “y“ is changed to “i“. For example, “happy – happier than – the happiest“.?
**A:** When an adjective ..., the “y“ is changed to “i“

**Q:** Give me an example, please.?
**A:** lucky – luckier than – the luckiest

**Q:** Give me an example of the word “though“, please.?
**A:** I didn’t like the food he cooked; I ate it though. Though she was feeling ill, she went on the business trip.

**Q:** When you have something unpleasant to do, do you delay doing it, or do?

**Q:** you do it at once without delay?
**A:** When I have ..., I delay doing it ~ When I have ..., I do it at once …

**Q:** Which is better though: to delay doing things or to do things at once?

**Q:** without delay?
**A:** It’s usually better to do things at once without delay

**Q:** When pupils are late for lessons, what kind of things have usually delayed?

**Q:** them?
**A:** When pupils …, the kind of things that have … are ...

**Q:** What must you do before you walk across the road?
**A:** I must look both ways before I ... I can see ... across the street from this window

**Q:** If you can’t get across a river by bridge, how can you get across?
**A:** If you can’t …, you can get across by boat or by swimming across

**Q:** If you were walking in a forest and suddenly came across a tiger, what’d?

**Q:** you do?
**A:** If I were ..., I’d ...

**Q:** If a lift isn’t working, what do we have to do?
**A:** If a lift …, we have to take the stairs

**Q:** Do you think it’s possible to tell a person’s character just by looking at?

**Q:** their face?
**A:** Yes, I think ... ~ No, I don’t think ...

**Q:** Can we often tell where somebody comes from by their accent?
**A:** Yes, we can …

**Q:** When you arrive at a hotel, where do you check in?
**A:** When you arrive … at the reception desk And what does the receptionist hand you when you’ve finished checking

**Q:** in?
**A:** The receptionist hands you the key to the room when … On the day you leave a hotel, what time do you usually have to check out

**Q:** by?
**A:** On the day … by noon

**Q:** some of it onto the plane with you?
**A:** At the airport, I usually check in all my luggage ~ At the airport, I usually carry some of my luggage onto the plane with me

**Q:** If someone says things about you which are not true, do you think it’s?

**Q:** better to defend yourself or just keep quiet?
**A:** If someone says things about me ..., I think …

**Q:** On what occasions do we say “Congratulations!” to people?
**A:** We say … when they pass an exam, get married, have a baby etc.

**Q:** and “I should study“?
**A:** The difference between those two sentences is that “I must study“ means that I have no alternative, whereas “I should study“ means I have alternatives but that studying is the right thing for me to do.

**Q:** 2) What can we use instead of “would be able“ in conditional sentences?

**Q:** Give me an example.?
**A:** We can use “could” instead of “would be able“ in conditional sentences. For example, “If I was a bird, I could fly”.

**Q:** 3) What’s the difference between “still“ and “yet“?
**A:** The difference between “still“ and “yet“ is that we use “still“ for something that is in progress at the moment, whereas we use “yet“ for something that has not begun or happened. We generally use “still” in positive sentences, whereas we generally use “yet” in questions and negative sentences. 4) What are the two ways of forming the 2nd conditional with the verb

**Q:** “to be”?
**A:** The two ways of forming the 2nd conditional with the verb “to be” are “if I was” and “if I were”. 5) What’s the difference between “for“ and “since“? Give me some examples. The difference between “for“ and “since“ is that we use the word “for“ when we say a period of time, whereas we use the word “since“ when we say the time at which a period began. For example, “for half an hour”; “for two weeks”; “since six o’clock”; “since last June”. 6) When do we use the past continuous? Give me an example. We use the past continuous for an action that was in progress at a particular time in the past. For example, “I was speaking English at this time yesterday“. 7) What is the most common use of the past continuous? Give me an example. The most common use of the past continuous is to say that an action was in progress when another action happened. For example, “I was cooking lunch when she arrived“.

**Q:** 8) What does the active voice communicate?
**A:** The active voice communicates that the subject does the action.

**Q:** 9) What does the passive voice communicate?
**A:** The passive voice communicates that the subject receives the action.

**Q:** 10) How do we form the passive voice?
**A:** We form the passive voice with the verb “to be“ and a past participle. 11) Change this sentence into the passive voice: “We are going to write the email”. “The email is going to be written by us”.

**Q:** 12) What are the two contracted forms of “you are not“?
**A:** The two contracted forms of “you are not” are “you aren’t“ and “you’re not“. 13) What three things can the words “in spite of” and “despite” be followed by? Give me an example of each. “In spite of” and “despite” can be followed by a noun, or “-ing”, or “the fact that ...”. For example, “In spite of her illness, she went to work”; “Despite being ill, she went to work”; “In spite of the fact that she was ill, she went to work”. 14) When do we use a reflexive pronoun? Give me an example. We use a reflexive pronoun when the subject and the object are the same person or thing. For example, “I looked at myself in the mirror”.

**Q:** 15) What are the reflexive pronouns?
**A:** The reflexive pronouns are “myself“, “yourself“, “himself“, “herself“, “itself“, “oneself“, “ourselves“, “yourselves“, and “themselves“.

**Q:** 16) What is the most common way to communicate purpose in English?

**Q:** Give me an example.?
**A:** The most common way to communicate purpose in English is by using the infinitive with “to”. For example, “I went to the hospital to see a doctor”.

**Q:** 17) What’s the difference between “to point at“ and “to point out“?
**A:** The difference between “to point at“ and “to point out“ is that we use “to point at“ for the action of pointing the finger at an object, whereas “to point out“ means to indicate something among different things.

**Q:** 18) What do the words “may“ and “might“ express?
**A:** The words “may” and “might” express the idea of “perhaps”.

**Q:** 19) What’s the difference between “may“ and “might“?
**A:** The difference between “may“ and “might“ is that we cannot use “may“ in the 2nd conditional. 20) What does it mean when we add the word “back“ to a verb? Give me some examples. When we add the word “back“ to a verb it means “to return“. For example, “give back“, “go back“, “pay back“ etc. 21) When do we use the past perfect? Give me an example. We use the past perfect when we are thinking about time before and up to another point in the past. For example, “Mary could not enter her flat yesterday because she had lost her key”. 22) Give me an example of the word “own“ after a possessive adjective. “This is my own book”.

**Q:** 23) Where do the words “as well“, “too“ and “also“ usually go?
**A:** The words “as well” and “too” go at the end of the sentence, but the word “also” usually goes after the first auxiliary verb. 24) Give me an example of “also“ when there is no auxiliary verb. I love coffee and I also love tea. 25) When do we use the future continuous? Give me an example. We use the future continuous for an action that will be in progress at a particular time in the future. For example, “I will be working at this time tomorrow”. 26) What’s the difference between “allow“ and “let“? Give me an example of each. The difference between “allow“ and “let“ is that “allow“ has the infinitive with “to“ after it, whereas “let“ has the infinitive without “to“ after it. For example, “The doctor allowed me to change my appointment“ and “The doctor let me change my appointment“. 27) What’s the most common way to speak about our habits? Give me an example. The most common way to speak about our habits is to use the present simple or past simple. For example, “I play football every weekend“; “I always studied hard before exams at university“.

**Q:** 28) What’s the difference between “travel“ and “journey“?
**A:** The difference between “travel” and “journey” is that we generally use “travel“ as a verb and “journey“ as a noun. 29) What’s the difference between the words “remember“ and

**Q:** “remind“?
**A:** The difference between the words “remember“ and “remind“ is that we remember something ourselves, without help, whereas, if we forget something, somebody reminds us. In other words, they remember for us. 30) What’s the difference between “to“ and “at“? Give me an example of each. The difference between “to” and “at” is that we generally use “to“ when we are moving in the direction of something, or somewhere, and “at“ when we are there. For example, “I’m going to the table. Now, I’m at the table“. 31) When do we use the 3rd conditional? Give me an example. We use the 3rd conditional when we are imagining something in the past that did not really happen. For example, “If I had not come to school last week, I would have stayed at home”. 32) In the 3rd conditional, what do we put after the word “would“? Give me some examples. In the 3rd conditional, we put the word “have“ and the past participle after the word “would“. For example, “he would have slept”; “she would have written”; “they would have eaten”. 33) Give me an example of an adverb formed from an adjective. An example of an adverb formed from an adjective is “badly“. 34) How do we form the possessive case of a plural noun which already ends in “s“? Give me an example. We form the possessive case of a plural noun which already ends in “s” just by adding an apostrophe, but no “s“. For example, “The girls’ coats“. “they“, “them“ etc. to talk about just one person when we don’t know if the person is a man or woman. For example, “There is somebody at the door. Go and see what they want”. The grammatical difference between “would rather” and “would prefer” is that we put the infinitive without “to“ after “would rather“, future simple are to communicate that we have just this moment decided to do something, and to make a prediction. For example, “Sorry I forgot to bring the money for you; “to be going to“ are to make a prediction using information we already have, and to communicate a future intention. For example, “Be careful; that wine glass is going to fall off the

**Q:** the present simple tense?
**A:** We use the auxiliary verb “do“ in a positive sentence with the present simple tense when we want to be emphatic. 40) When do we usually use the emphatic “do“? Give me an example. We usually use the emphatic “do” when we want to deny something that someone has said because we know it is not true. For example, if somebody says “You don’t eat enough vegetables“, I can reply “Yes I do eat enough vegetables!“ 41) What is another use of the emphatic “do“? Give me an example. Another use of the emphatic “do” is in an exclamation. For example, “I do hate noisy children“.

**Q:** 42) How do we form the future perfect?
**A:** We form the future perfect with the verb “to have“ and a past participle. 43) When do we use the future perfect? Give me an example. We use the future perfect when we are thinking about time before and up to a point in the future. For example, “When you arrive at my house, I’ll have cooked dinner”. 44) Give me an example of direct speech. Mr Brown said “I like warm weather“. 45) Give me an example of indirect speech. Mr Brown said that he liked warm weather. 46) What do we do with the tenses when we change direct speech into indirect speech? Give me an example. When we change direct speech into indirect speech, we generally move the verb into the past. For example: The teacher said “The room is large“. The teacher said that the room was large.

**Q:** 47) What do we do if a verb is already in the past?
**A:** If a verb is already in the past, it often remains unchanged, but sometimes we put it further into the past. 48) What do we do with the word “will“ when we change direct speech into indirect speech? Give me an example. When we change direct speech into indirect speech, we change the word “will“ to “would“. For example: She said that John would go to London next year.

**Q:** 49) What’s the difference between the words “what“ and “whatever“?

**Q:** Give me an example of “whatever”. 50) Tell me four common ways in which we can make a suggestion. 51) How do we make the imperative in English? Give me some examples. 52) How do we make a negative imperative? Give me some examples. 53) How do we form the perfect continuous tenses? Give me an example. 54) When do we use the present perfect continuous? Give me an example. 55) When do we use the past perfect continuous? Give me an example. living in this house for one month when I bought my new bed”. 56) When do we use the future perfect continuous? Give me an example. been living in this house for eight months at the end of this year”.?
**A:** The difference between the words “what” and “whatever” is that we use the word “what“ in a limited sense, whereas we use the word “whatever“ in a more unlimited sense. For example, “If I could buy whatever I wanted, I’d buy …”. Four common ways in which we can make a suggestion are: Shall we wait for him? Let’s wait for him. How (or What) about waiting for him? Why don’t we wait for him? We make the imperative in English by using the infinitive without “to“. For example, “Give me the book!“; “Do it now!“; “Have a nice day!“ We make a negative imperative by putting the word “don’t“ before the infinitive. For example, “Don’t give him the book!“; “Don’t be late!“; “Don’t forget to email me!“ We form the perfect continuous tenses by using the verb “have“, the word “been“, and the present participle of the main verb. For example, “I have been studying for two hours“. We use the present perfect continuous to say how long an action has been in progress so far. For example, “I have been living in this house for six months so far”. We use the past perfect continuous to communicate the duration of an action up to a particular point in the past. For example, “I had been We use the future perfect continuous to communicate the duration of an action up to a particular point in the future. For example, “I will have

**Q:** 57) When do we use a tail question?
**A:** We use a tail question when we believe something is true, and we want somebody to confirm that we are right. 58) How do we form a tail question? Give me an example. We form a tail question by repeating the first auxiliary verb from the main part of the sentence, and putting it in question form. For example, “He is going to go out tonight, isn’t he?” 59) When there is no auxiliary verb in the main part of the sentence,

**Q:** which verb do we use in the tail question? Give me an example.?
**A:** When there is no auxiliary verb in the main part of the sentence, we use “do“ in the tail question. For example, “They come every Thursday, don’t they?”

**Q:** 60) What does the structure “to have something done“ communicate?

**Q:** Give me an example. 61) What’s the most important difference between “tell“ and “say“? Give me an example. She told him that it was important. She said it was important. 62) After the verb “say“, how is it possible to indicate the person we are?
**A:** The structure “to have something done“ communicates that we don’t do the action ourselves, but that somebody else does it for us. For example, “I don’t cut my hair myself; I have my hair cut by a hairdresser“. The most important difference between “tell“ and “say“ is that after “tell“ we indicate the person we are speaking to, but after “say“ we usually do not. For example:

**Q:** speaking to?
**A:** After the verb “say“, it is possible to indicate the person we are speaking to by using the word “to“.

**Q:** 63) For direct speech, which verb do we normally use: “say“ or “tell“?

**Q:** Give me an example.?
**A:** For direct speech, we normally use the verb “say“. For example: She said “I’m hungry”.

**Q:** 64) What are the two basic types of auxiliary verb in English?
**A:** The two basic types of auxiliary verb in English are primary auxiliaries and modal auxiliaries.

**Q:** 65) What are the primary auxiliaries?
**A:** The primary auxiliaries are the verbs “be“, “have“ and “do“. 66) When do we use the auxiliary “be“? Give me an example. We use the auxiliary “be“ to make the continuous tenses and the passive voice. For example, “I am speaking English now” and “This book was printed in England”. 67) When do we use the auxiliary “have“? Give me an example. We use the auxiliary “have“ for the perfect tenses. For example, “He has gone to Scotland”. 68) When do we use the auxiliary “do“? Give me an example. We use the auxiliary “do“ for the present simple and past simple. For example, “I don’t speak Spanish“ and “Did she eat the pasta?“

**Q:** 69) What are the ten common modals?
**A:** The ten common modals are “can“, “could“, “will“, “would“, “may“, “might“, “shall“, should“, “must“ and “ought“. 70) What do we put after a modal? Give me an example. We put the infinitive without “to“ after a modal. For example, “I must send this email today”. 71) Which modal is the only exception to this rule? Give me an example. “Ought“ is the only exception to this rule. For example, “I ought to see a doctor”. 72) Give me a sentence containing the words “by the time“. For example, “By the time she gets here, dinner will be ready“.

**Q:** 73) What does “by the time“ mean in that sentence?
**A:** “By the time“ in that sentence means at some point between now and when she gets here.

**Q:** 74) What’s the difference between the words “older“ and “elder“?
**A:** The difference between the words “older” and “elder” is that we generally use the word “elder” when speaking about people in the same family, and we cannot say “elder than” 75) When do we double the final consonant of an adjective in forming its comparative and its superlative? Give me an example. We double the final consonant of an adjective in forming its comparative and its superlative when it ends in a single consonant preceded by a single vowel. For example, “thin – thinner than – the thinnest“. 76) What happens when an adjective ends in a consonant plus “y”? Give me an example. When an adjective ends in a consonant plus “y”, the “y” is changed to “i”. For example, “lucky – luckier than – the luckiest“.

### Dictation
> The arrow/ flew through the air/ and hit the tree/ in the middle./ He looked very strange;/ he had fair hair/ but a dark beard./ I do not know/ how much they gave him,/ but it was a large amount./ She goes to the same café/ every morning for a snack./ Poor John was not invited/ to Mary’s birthday party./ I am sorry,/ but I have already thrown/ the old cooker away;/ it was too dangerous to keep.

- **English**
- **Polish Vocabulary Book**
- **Stage 6**
- **situation...................................sytuacja**
- **425 deny....................................zaprzeczać**
- **thirst.................................... pragnienie**
- **425 exclamation. ................. wykrzyknienie**
- **hotter and hotter......................... coraz**
- **425 stress...............................nacisk, akcent**
- **. ............................... cieplejszy (cieplej)**
- **more and more.................coraz więcej**
- **ready........................................ gotowy**
- **marriage............................małżeństwo**
- **427 hit............................................. uderzyć**
- **whereabouts. .......... w którym miejscu**
- **427 arrest.................................. aresztować**
- **describe. ................................opisywać**
- **description..................................... opis**
- **just. .......................................zaraz, tuż**
- **. .............................. wesołe miasteczko**
- **correct.................................. poprawny**
- **427 fairly.............................. umiarkowanie**
- **incorrect.......................... niepoprawny**
- **427 just. ................................. sprawiedliwy**
- **as soon as...............................gdy tylko**
- **427 moderate....................... umiarkowany**
- **over......................................skończony**
- **427 unfair.......................... niesprawiedliwy**
- **strange...................................... dziwny**
- **428 pleasure. .......................... przyjemność**
- **shut........................................ zamknąć**
- **. .................................... doświadczenie**
- **shut up.............................. zamknąć się**
- **429 discover......................................odkryć**
- **be quiet.................................. być cicho**
